- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C02-3|MT131-C02-3]] — An attempt to remove Fed governor Lisa Cook could give the administration about five of seven board seats, raising Fed-independence risk.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C02-X2|MT131-C02-X2]] — Sudden US policy announcements (e.g. the end-July 2025 copper tariff news) can reverse established trends and hurt trend followers at short notice.

## [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323]]
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C02-5|MT132-C02-5]] — A new Trump administration could quickly end US support for Ukraine, which Europe is not prepared for.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C07-1|MT132-C07-1]] — America First policy favours small-cap domestic stocks, so Russell outperforms while the S&P grinds higher and Nvidia lags.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C08-1|MT132-C08-1]] — Reflexivity: the Fed's rate-cut talk lowers long rates, which is stimulus (sequestered housing demand released), making inflation and growth stickier than expected.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C08-X3|MT132-C08-X3]] — Dovish Fed talk late in the year lowers long-term yields and produces a spring economic push even if the promised cuts never come, so the narrative alone acts as stimulus.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C10-3|MT132-C10-3]] — In populist periods, policymakers pump markets before elections via Fed policy and pay the bill in non-election years.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C14-1|MT132-C14-1]] — Because the modern Fed will use inflation to avoid depression, the current populist period is more likely inflationary than deflationary.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C14-2|MT132-C14-2]] — The Fed has two mandates and one tool and structural inflation from borrowing against the future, so its put is less reliable and volatility will rise.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C16-X11|MT132-C16-X11]] — Bitcoin's upside is capped long term because it has no sovereign sponsor and states (US, China) will restrict it if it threatens their monetary power such as the dollar's exorbitant privilege; Jim is skeptical of extreme targets like $13 million.

## [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini]]
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C01-1|MT133-C01-1]] — In 2010-2020 the central-bank put made bad news bullish for assets, compressing volatility and making life difficult for macro traders.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C01-2|MT133-C01-2]] — The 2022 inflation shock broke the central-bank put: banks had to tighten (fastest hike cycle ex-Volcker, largest real rate rise), so stocks fell and macro funds profited.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C01-3|MT133-C01-3]] — In Q1 2024 equities rose in a straight line from about 4200 to 5200 on the S&P with an information ratio near 4 versus a normal 0.6-0.7, after the Fed turned dovish in December and volatility hit lows.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C01-X1|MT133-C01-X1]] — The COVID shock was external but triggered a massive central-bank reaction and asset inflation; after years of loose monetary and fiscal policy, goods and consumer inflation arrived in 2022, so loose policy sowed the inflation shock that ended the 'bad news is good news' regime.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C01-X2|MT133-C01-X2]] — In December 2023 the Fed and nearly every developed central bank (all except the BoJ) turned dovish while growth was decent, a benign backdrop for equities, and markets had already rallied in anticipation of it.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C02-3|MT133-C02-3]] — The Fed's 2% target is on core PCE (excluding energy and food), so core PCE is the inflation series to follow.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C03-1|MT133-C03-1]] — Core PCE monthly prints annualising to about 5% (inflation bounced in early 2024) would trigger another Fed hike; this is a main left-tail risk though not the base case.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-2|MT133-C04-2]] — Stock-bond correlation is negative when growth shocks dominate (rates fall on bad news) and positive when inflation is high (bad shocks hit both stocks and bonds).
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-5|MT133-C04-5]] — In 2022 the macro book was short rates, short stocks and commodities and long the dollar because the Fed was the most aggressive; in 2024 conviction was lower but still short rates with US curve steepeners and short Japanese rates.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-X1|MT133-C04-X1]] — A macro book's key inputs are growth, inflation and the Fed and other central banks; central-bank policy is the part it can anticipate well, and it performs best when central banks are actively changing policy.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-X3|MT133-C04-X3]] — In 2022 the macro book paired short global rates (on the view that rates needed to rise 400-500bp) with short stocks and short commodities and long the dollar, because large rate rises usually push stocks down and the Fed was the most aggressive central bank.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C05-1|MT133-C05-1]] — A strong US economy limits Fed cuts while the ECB cuts independently, supporting a long dollar view.

## [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367]]
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C08-2|MT136-C08-2]] — Let-it-run-hot view: political pressure keeps rates lower than warranted, lifting equity valuations and gold/crypto, with a bond tantrum later rather than sooner; the president is also structurally short the dollar.

## [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-2|MT138-C02-2]] — Latin America, left for dead for 25 years, has political shifts (Argentina's Milei, elections in Peru, Colombia, Chile, Brazil) bringing better policies, with returns across equities, currencies, credit and high rates.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-X1|MT138-C02-X1]] — Citrone assesses countries like companies, starting with management quality and policy; the political change in Latin America (Milei in Argentina, spreading across the region) is the core of his thesis there.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-X4|MT138-C02-X4]] — Karniol-Tambour likes Brazilian and Mexican currencies as part of Latin American exposure, and sees capital staying at home as the US pushes self-sufficiency.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C03-2|MT138-C03-2]] — Policies imply some slowdown and some inflation in the US; most portfolios are not prepared and have shrinking liquid allocations to respond or take advantage of pricing opportunities.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C03-4|MT138-C03-4]] — Tariff pain is expected in 2025 and fiscal stimulation and reshoring in 2026; faster resolution of uncertainty speeds reshoring.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C03-5|MT138-C03-5]] — Prefer non-US government bonds because other central banks can ease more straightforwardly while the Fed faces an inflation impulse from tariffs.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C03-X1|MT138-C03-X1]] — The May 2025 tariff de-escalation removed the tail risk of massive factory shutdowns and empty shelves, but a slowdown and some inflation still lie ahead, which most portfolios are not prepared for.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C04-1|MT138-C04-1]] — Tariff effects are inflationary in the US (more so if supply constraints) but deflationary abroad (weaker currencies, lower energy), so the Fed cannot ease easily while other central banks can, and little non-US easing is priced.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C04-5|MT138-C04-5]] — Short Japan and US rates (yields higher); play falling rates in Brazil, Mexico, Argentina, Turkey and perhaps the UK where rates can fall hundreds to thousands of basis points.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C04-X1|MT138-C04-X1]] — The Fed's hope is that tariff inflation is a one-off price-level shift like a VAT rise, but if tariffs come with supply disruption it looks more like Covid, with demand intact and supply short, and persistent inflation; this uncertainty stops the Fed from easing.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C06-X1|MT138-C06-X1]] — Institutions held fixed income as a recession hedge (central banks ease when growth falls); when rates were at zero that role seemed pointless, so money moved to private credit with equity-like risk, hollowing out bond-selection capability.

## [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,]]
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C02-1|MT139-C02-1]] — After the 1987 crash the Fed eased liquidity massively and markets recovered quickly, so heavy liquidity injection marked a buying opportunity.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C03-1|MT139-C03-1]] — In 2022 the Fed cannot rescue markets as in 1987 because inflation forces it to tighten and withdraw liquidity.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C03-4|MT139-C03-4]] — Core inflation peaked in mid-2022 after three monthly declines but will not fall fast enough to stop Fed tightening; energy and food are headline risks.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C04-X1|MT139-C04-X1]] — Citrone's early view that Shanghai's Pudong high-rises were a ghost-town disaster was wrong within a year because the state brought people in, an analogy that state-directed development can fill apparent overbuild and that vacancy alone is a poor basis for a China bear case.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C09-X1|MT139-C09-X1]] — The two swing factors Citrone identifies for the rest of 2022 are whether the Ukraine war is resolved (still a quagmire; a resolution would be a big market event) and who leads China after the autumn party congress.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C10-3|MT139-C10-3]] — Brazil offers rate-cut upside (rates 14%), equities and currency carry (12% net yield) as political risk is stable in the 2022 election; Argentina could be best market next year after market-oriented election change.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C11-1|MT139-C11-1]] — Japan's yield curve control cap at 25 bps cannot hold with global inflation; short JGBs, long end gains of 50-75 bps; developed sovereign bonds were shorts generally.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C11-X1|MT139-C11-X1]] — African commodity strength is hard to capture directly because of political chaos and weak governance, so Citrone prefers Western companies with African operations and sees little to buy in South Africa.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C13-2|MT139-C13-2]] — Central banks coordinate and share ideas via the BIS, so policymaker contacts help anticipate their stances.

## [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)]]
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C04-1|MT141-C04-1]] — Bad policy creates good opportunities: after the S&L bust a regulator overreacted with supervisory tightening (write down even current loans), producing an opposite misguided reaction and mispriced bank stocks.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C06-1|MT141-C06-1]] — UK mortgages floated with the base rate, so defending the pound in the ERM by raising rates would bankrupt the voting public; the policy was therefore politically unsustainable and the British and German rate needs were incompatible.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C06-X2|MT141-C06-X2]] — Divergence of cycles inside a common-rate regime makes a peg untenable: the rate-sensitive UK housing market weakened over summer 1992 (after the pound had first strengthened on Major's surprise win), showing UK and German rate needs differed.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C09-3|MT141-C09-3]] — Setup for the 2012-13 yen short: yen was the most overvalued currency on PPP after years of disinflation while Abe's three-arrow plan targeted 2% inflation, 2% growth in two years.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-1|MT141-C10-1]] — Conviction in the Abenomics trade came from verifying the mechanism: Abe could reconstitute the Bank of Japan board and get a reflationary governor, making the policy change feasible.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C10-7|MT141-C10-7]] — A policy regime change does not need to succeed to create a large price move; the market reaction itself is the trade (the market of the lifetime).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C12-7|MT141-C12-7]] — Best investments detect when authorities will reverse an unsustainable policy; assume they brake before the wall (though sometimes they hit it).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C13-1|MT141-C13-1]] — Policy is personnel: policymakers want to be judged well and are open to external pressure, so watching them over time makes it easy to anticipate when they slam the brakes.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C13-X1|MT141-C13-X1]] — Oct 2024 prediction: Marine Le Pen becomes president of France within 18 to 24 months because the left alternative is worse, part of a populist backlash to trade and immigration in Europe.

## [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King]]
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C04-1|MT142-C04-1]] — Decisive catalyst for the yen short: Abe's reflationary platform plus three open BoJ board seats let the Prime Minister reconstitute a deflationist board, including the governor.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C04-2|MT142-C04-2]] — Policy changes can drive huge price moves regardless of whether the policy works; as policymaker the speaker now judges what delivers rather than what is priced.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C04-4|MT142-C04-4]] — Even as a policymaker the market carries information; his past work was guessing what policymakers would do, now he asks what can, should and will be done and how the market will react.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C05-X1|MT142-C05-X1]] — March 2026 view: the US war aims are to destroy Iran's missile, manufacturing, air and naval capability and its power projection, and the operation is running well ahead of schedule, which bears on the duration (not the level) of the oil shock.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C06-2|MT142-C06-2]] — Hormuz tanker flow is expected to recover once US Navy or an international coalition can escort ships after air control, since the straits are not mined.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C06-3|MT142-C06-3]] — A government-backed insurance product (DFC, $20bn) replaces private cover that Lloyd's withdraws under force majeure, so tankers can keep moving.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C07-4|MT142-C07-4]] — The Fed's reaction to an oil shock depends on whether it lifts inflation expectations and on how long it slows growth: potential energy that can bounce back, or a permanent slowdown.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C08-X1|MT142-C08-X1]] — Fed versus BoE design: the Fed wears many hats (monetary and regulatory) with regional presidents and a large board narrowed to voters, whereas the BoE separates its MPC from its operating committee; he rejects returning to government-set rates (pre-1997 UK) and sees the inflation target as accountability by letter.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C09-1|MT142-C09-1]] — Extraordinary measures should only occur in extraordinary times: the BoE stabilised gilts in spring 2020 and stepped back, whereas the Fed kept buying for years, which helped cause the 2022-23 inflation.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C09-X2|MT142-C09-X2]] — The Treasury Secretary role matters more than the Fed chair now because it covers dollar dominance, the sanctions regime and managing debt with Congress, which he sees as an extraordinary-moment priority.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C10-1|MT142-C10-1]] — After the Supreme Court bars IEEPA tariffs, the administration rebuilds the tariff wall under Section 301 (challenged over 4,000 times) and a 150-day Section 122 tariff, now a global 10%, with trade deals kept.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C11-X1|MT142-C11-X1]] — Policy shocks are cushioned by pre-announcing the fallback plan: since the Supreme Court hearing the administration told markets and allies how it would rebuild the tariff wall if it lost, so the ruling carried no surprise.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C11-X2|MT142-C11-X2]] — March 2026 view: G7 leaders and Gulf states back the US campaign and many countries offered minesweepers, so an international coalition to keep the Strait of Hormuz open is likely, supporting a shorter disruption.

## [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299]]
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C02-X1|MT143-C02-X1]] — June 2024 view: the strong US jobs report is further evidence against the claim that rates will fall sharply and inflation will quickly return to 2%, so he stays cautious on rate cuts.

## [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312]]
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C04-1|MT144-C04-1]] — Sept 2024 view: US unemployment data (due that day) would set the Fed path; soft data since April; he expected 25bp cuts at each of the three remaining meetings.

## [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C01-2|MT145-C01-2]] — Since 1987 (then 2001-03 and the GFC) US political and Fed intervention in markets has grown after each crisis, changing how markets behave.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C02-1|MT145-C02-1]] — With market cap/GDP (Buffett ratio) over 200% vs a 70% norm, the stock market drives the economy, so Fed and politicians act whenever it corrects (a Fed put).
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C02-4|MT145-C02-4]] — Political suppression of volatility, inflation and drawdowns shortens and reverses trends, which has been a headwind for trend followers.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C02-X1|MT145-C02-X1]] — With the stock market above 200% of GDP, each 1% equity return adds about 2% of GDP in new liquidity (a wealth effect), so equity prices drive the economy and policy leans against every equity drawdown.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C05-4|MT145-C05-4]] — With a Fed put the tail hedge is free, so convergence/short-vol trades earn steady profit for a long time while risk builds unseen.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C06-X1|MT145-C06-X1]] — Because financialisation means a 20% equity fall hits GDP, the Fed must ease into it, and with fiscal dominance already present this points to inflation as the endgame rather than a clean deflationary bust.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C09-5|MT145-C09-5]] — Governments successively suppressed volatility in commodities, then FX, then fixed income, with inflation now limiting rates and stocks the last pinned asset, which pushed CTAs to drift.

## [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour]]
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C03-3|MT147-C03-3]] — Rising rates and falling asset prices make households unlikely to lever up or save less again, so debt cannot fill the gap left by fiscal support.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C03-6|MT147-C03-6]] — Corporate taxes and antitrust regulation are at multi-decade lows, so political pressure on inequality points toward higher taxes and tougher regulation, a profit headwind.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C03-X1|MT147-C03-X1]] — The transfer-fuelled spending boom created inflation, which forced central banks to raise rates, and that rate rise in turn closes the borrowing channel that was supporting profits, so the stimulus sows the end of its own profit support.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C03-X2|MT147-C03-X2]] — The damage from rising rates and falling asset prices to household borrowing and spending operates with a lag and has not yet shown up in spending data, so a spending slowdown is still ahead.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C05-1|MT147-C05-1]] — Equities have only repriced for higher rates, not for lower profits, so no earnings slowdown is priced in.

## [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C01-1|MT148-C01-1]] — The Fed's slow recognition of inflation (QE until March 2022, no verbal pivot until November) was costly because assets bought in that period will lose money.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C01-X1|MT148-C01-X1]] — His 2021 call: when monetary and fiscal policy are historically out of step with the economy and there is a raging mania across all assets, investors disregard looming inflation, so inflation and an asset-bubble burst follow; he treats the policy-versus-circumstances gap as the core signal.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C02-1|MT148-C02-1]] — Soft landings are rare (two or three in history) and none occurred once inflation exceeded 4.5%, so a soft landing is a long shot.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C02-2|MT148-C02-2]] — Historically inflation above 5% has never fallen unless Fed funds exceeded CPI; with CPI at 8% that is unlikely given the asset bubble.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C02-X2|MT148-C02-X2]] — The Fed is far behind the inflation curve: even its projected policy rate of about 2% sits far below 8% CPI, so a lot of tightening is still needed against a broad asset bubble.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-1|MT148-C05-1]] — His traditional bear-market playbook is long Treasuries, which may fail with 8% inflation and 3% yields.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C05-2|MT148-C05-2]] — Positioning in the past 6-8 months: short fixed income, short stocks, long oil, copper and gold.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C10-2|MT148-C10-2]] — Easing with no domestic crisis, in 1999, supported the tech rally, so he went long with young specialists.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C11-1|MT148-C11-1]] — Rising oil, rates and dollar together have been bad for forward earnings; a regression by Ed Hyman implied -35% earnings versus consensus +18% in 2000.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C11-2|MT148-C11-2]] — He took a huge long Treasury position in 2000 on the view that tightening would reverse into recession and made 40% in Q4.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C16-X1|MT148-C16-X1]] — A year and a half before the conversation he put 70% on inflation and 30% on deflation as the end-state, reasoning that inflation had not appeared earlier only because policy was at the zero bound and that deflation follows asset bubbles.

## [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C02-1|MT149-C02-1]] — The Fed kept buying $120bn a month at zero rates into a booming recovery, fuelling inflation to 9% and speculative excess in crypto and equities.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C02-2|MT149-C02-2]] — Two rules: bad economies follow easy-money asset bubbles, and do not fight the Fed. He sees the biggest and broadest asset bubble he has studied meeting a big rate hike.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C02-3|MT149-C02-3]] — Soft landings are rare since 1950 and only followed proactive Fed policy, and this Fed was reactive, so odds of a soft landing are low.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C03-3|MT149-C03-3]] — Inflation should fall to 3-3.5% within 6-9 months, after which the Fed's reaction is the key unknown, and an Arthur Burns-style early pause could bring stagflation.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C03-4|MT149-C03-4]] — If the Fed eases too early at 3-3.5% inflation as Arthur Burns did, a stagflationary environment could follow within a few years.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C03-X2|MT149-C03-X2]] — Three years out he can argue for either 8% inflation or deflation, so the long-run inflation outcome is a wide two-sided range that depends on how policymakers respond after the bubble bursts.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C03-X4|MT149-C03-X4]] — Monetary tightening works with long and variable lags; a year after the first hikes the full effect on inflation and the economy has not yet arrived, which supports expecting inflation to fall to about 3-3.5% and a slowdown later.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C04-3|MT149-C04-3]] — Commodities did well in the 1970s and 2000s equity bubble bursts when policy easing followed, so cutting commodities before a hard landing is not reliable.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C06-1|MT149-C06-1]] — There is a 5-10% chance Fed independence is lost if a hard landing makes the Fed take the blame for its record.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C10-1|MT149-C10-1]] — Volcker's deliberate 1982 recession cleaned out inflation and led to 20-30 years of prosperity, so a flush-out is the constructive path.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C13-1|MT149-C13-1]] — Policy preference for a carbon tax over government-directed climate intervention.

## [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347]]
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-5|MT150-C04-5]] — Consensus expects a deep recession with disinflation; the speaker expects a mild, slow recession with hot inflation, i.e. stagflation of the high-inflation kind.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-6|MT150-C04-6]] — The Fed holds because inflation effects from tariffs are certain while the growth effect is uncertain.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C05-R1|MT150-C05-R1]] — Treasury's short-dated issuance under Yellen is explained as election-period incentives, not incompetence; Trump's pressure on the Fed is the same behaviour, so incentives predict policy under either party.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C06-1|MT150-C06-1]] — The Fed is stuck between inflation and growth, with a shrinking set of choices, as Burns was in the 1970s, and populist politics add to it.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C06-2|MT150-C06-2]] — The administration's early supply-side plan assumed a market drop would make the Fed cut and lower the 10-year; it failed in a month, ending the plan.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C06-X1|MT150-C06-X1]] — Fed chairs squeezed between inflation and growth (Burns, Powell) get cast as fools, while Volcker looked heroic mainly because he had room to act decisively at the right time; the current Fed's set of choices keeps shrinking.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C06-X2|MT150-C06-X2]] — The supply-side plan failed politically as well as on rates: the first 100 days saw the worst approval rating since 1940, which pushed the administration back toward its populist base.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C07-X1|MT150-C07-X1]] — The administration was always an unstable equilibrium between supply-side business interests and populist protectionists; with the supply-side faction out and the populists in charge, investors should not expect a business-friendly policy mix.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C07-X2|MT150-C07-X2]] — The midterm incentive forces a pivot back to populist, bottom-income-friendly policy, so the political drift toward spending and away from supply-side policy is likely to persist.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C09-1|MT150-C09-1]] — The lag between rate rises and market damage lengthens after rates hit zero because borrowers locked in five-year financing; the later hit is bigger (reason for Shiller's smoothed 10-year CAPE).
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C11-1|MT150-C11-1]] — The administration's main inflation lever is oil, via US production and Saudi supply, so oil prices are pushed lower; early-2025 oil shorts were profitable.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C12-1|MT150-C12-1]] — Saudi/OPEC supply increases serve the long game and favour the US administration, so supply can be set outside normal supply-demand forces.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C17-5|MT150-C17-5]] — The administration pushes deal announcements to support the market, and a smaller-than-expected deal would pull risk forward.

## [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C01-2|MT151-C01-2]] — Financial conditions are a key market input; loose conditions (looser than when the Fed began tightening) keep the economy and inflation supported even after a rebound in the dollar and yields tightened them in recent weeks.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C01-5|MT151-C01-5]] — A 50bp cut while credit spreads are tight, gold at highs and equities rising eases conditions into strength and risks reigniting inflation.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C01-6|MT151-C01-6]] — A Trump win could raise animal spirits (deregulation) while tariffs are marginally inflationary and immigration restrictions cut labor supply, adding to inflation risk.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C02-1|MT151-C02-1]] — The Fed should avoid big mistakes (1970s, GFC, 2021 inflation) rather than fine-tune a soft landing; its obsession with a soft landing is a source of policy error.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C02-2|MT151-C02-2]] — Forward guidance removes optionality: a Fed that is guidance-dependent rather than data-dependent cannot easily reverse, which raises the chance of policy error.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C02-6|MT151-C02-6]] — If easing into a melt-up reignites inflation, the Fed must hike again, which is when guidance-bound policy would produce a trust crisis in Treasuries (best guess: a failed auction).
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C02-X1|MT151-C02-X1]] — He explains Fed behaviour by the Chair's wish to engineer a soft landing and repair his legacy after the 2021 error, not by politics; but the landing is only needed because the Fed let inflation rise from 2% to 9%, so fine-tuning risks a second mistake.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C03-3|MT151-C03-3]] — He was short bonds (about 25% of NAV in 10-year equivalents), shorted the day of the first Fed cut, and would size bigger if he saw confirmation.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C08-5|MT151-C08-5]] — Diverging economies (UK housing slump vs German reunification boom) require opposite interest rates, making a peg between them unsustainable.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-1|MT151-C09-1]] — On seeing a Bundesbank head's editorial saying the mark and pound should no longer be linked, he scaled to 100% of the fund because the cost remained about 0.5%.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-2|MT151-C09-2]] — The thesis for breaking the peg: one economy needs higher rates and the other lower, so the currencies cannot stay linked.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-5|MT151-C09-5]] — Large rate hikes to defend a peg failed because the economy was weak; hikes did not stop the break.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-6|MT151-C09-6]] — After the devaluation, he bought gilts (down 2 points, rates needed to be lower) and British stocks (exports); academic theory that weak currency forces rates up was rejected in this case.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C10-3|MT151-C10-3]] — The Swedish krona peg was a similar divergence-driven peg trade.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C11-2|MT151-C11-2]] — He realized Greenspan had eased because of the Asian crisis while the economy was strong, which fed the bubble, and pivoted to buy tech, finishing 1999 up about 42%.

## [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview]]
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C01-2|MT152-C01-2]] — The Fed's 'considerable period' guidance in 2003 after growth had turned ensured only a housing bubble that led to the crisis; policy kept easy too long after the cycle turned.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C01-3|MT152-C01-3]] — Counterfactual: if the Fed had cut and reversed policy 6-9 months earlier in 2007 when housing was clearly a disaster, there might have been a recession but not a financial crisis.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C03-1|MT152-C03-1]] — Normalization should be slow because rapid tightening risks recession followed by more extreme easing, which would make eventual normalization harder.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C03-3|MT152-C03-3]] — The Fed's reaction function is to keep hiking until something breaks or financial conditions tighten; market confusion arises because the Fed does not know the terminal rate.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C03-4|MT152-C03-4]] — Hiking should be done when financial conditions are booming; hiking while the S&P is down 13%, cyclicals down 25-40% and the balance sheet is shrinking is not 'sneaking one in'.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C04-1|MT152-C04-1]] — Markets react to the rate of change of tightening, and the US has not hiked into a market meltdown like this since Volcker (who had 12% inflation as justification).
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C04-4|MT152-C04-4]] — Forward guidance ties the Fed's hands; after the taper tantrum they stuck to a QE and rate schedule to protect credibility even as conditions boomed, and now are stuck with wrong guidance.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C05-X1|MT152-C05-X1]] — The Fed should decide on data: pausing because a President demands it and refusing to pause to avoid looking bullied are both political decisions, so the presidential pressure should be taken out of the equation when judging whether a pause is right.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C05-X2|MT152-C05-X2]] — Large-scale QE creates a path dependence: it is far easier to start than to unwind, so the new Fed chair inherits a balance sheet and expectations that make normalisation a very tough road.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C06-1|MT152-C06-1]] — Credibility comes from being data dependent: when conditions that justified a December hike (S&P rising, oil $85) have reversed, policy should change.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C06-3|MT152-C06-3]] — Most of his large profits came from central bank mistakes and their effect on liquidity (confirmed 'correct').
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C07-6|MT152-C07-6]] — In bear markets he made his money in Treasuries (e.g. the 2-year, 6.04% to 1.5-2% in 2000-01), because yields fall dramatically when the Fed eases.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C08-1|MT152-C08-1]] — He is long 2-, 5- and 10-year Treasuries even at 2.68% because of the risk-reward; if the Fed errs, 2-year yields could fall to 50-60bp with QE and zero rates again.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C09-3|MT152-C09-3]] — He is short financials because banks need rates to go up to do well but equities do not do well when rates rise; if rates go down his cloud names should do better.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C10-3|MT152-C10-3]] — Brexit pound: upside to 1.35 if no hard break, downside through the post-referendum low of 1.18 if hard Brexit and Corbyn gains; payoff binary (+10% or -10%) unlike 1992.

## [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets]]
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C03-1|MT153-C03-1]] — Free money squeezed the bond and currency profit centers that produced about 70% of his gains, forcing a refocus on equities.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C03-4|MT153-C03-4]] — Rates are too low for the conditions (robust growth, fiscal stimulus, unemployment under 4%); policy rate should be 4-5% rather than 1.75%.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C06-X1|MT153-C06-X1]] — The policy dilemma as framed: not raising rates lets asset prices keep inflating, and deflation (in Druckenmiller's earlier view) is caused by deflating an asset bubble that grew too large, so delay raises eventual bust risk, while raising rates risks defaults given the debt pile.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C07-1|MT153-C07-1]] — Fed policy rule: raise rates at every meeting (sneak one in) until financial disruption appears, then pause; avoid a predictable quarterly path.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C07-5|MT153-C07-5]] — The late-1990s blow-off was fueled by Fed cuts in autumn 1998 not by higher rates; Greenspan eased when nothing was wrong with the economy.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C07-X2|MT153-C07-X2]] — Whether markets handle each hike should be judged by broad financial-conditions measures (credit, EM, funding), not by a 5-10% equity correction; the central bank should look through ordinary equity pullbacks and stop only on a dramatic tightening of conditions.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C07-X3|MT153-C07-X3]] — With the US at 3.8% unemployment and rates still very low, the ECB is the most egregious case of too-low rates; years of negative rates imply large hidden malinvestment and rot in European bank balance sheets.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C08-1|MT153-C08-1]] — Not hiking in the late 1990s was the original sin: Nasdaq at 125x earnings, the dot-com bust, then offset by housing, setting up the later crisis.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C08-3|MT153-C08-3]] — Tightening continues until stress reaches developed markets; EM bombs do not stop it, and continuing tightening keeps setting off EM bombs.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C11-2|MT153-C11-2]] — He bought Treasuries as Greenspan kept giving hawkish speeches despite no price confirmation, because fundamental conviction was strong; this was one of his best bets.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C13-1|MT153-C13-1]] — The BoJ's shift is driven by damage to banks from ultra-low rates rather than economics, which also affects the ECB banks and political outcomes.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C13-4|MT153-C13-4]] — In 2003 policy rate of 1% against 9% nominal growth plus the 'considerable period' guidance fueled malinvestment and subprime; the seeds of 2008.

## [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-1|MT154-C01-1]] — Point72 puts recession odds at about 45% (May 2025) with significant growth slowing from tariffs, though the odds fell after the China tariff reversal.
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-2|MT154-C01-2]] — The effective US tariff rate is expected near 14-17% (cut from a 16-20% expectation) after the China tariff retreat.
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-3|MT154-C01-3]] — The Fed will not cut quickly when growth slows because tariff inflation keeps it cautious.
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-6|MT154-C01-6]] — After the policy pivot, downside is limited to the 10-15% below the April lows and equities should trade in a range for a while.
- [[MT154 - Steve Cohen and Jawad Mian at Sohn 2025#^MT154-C01-R1|MT154-C01-R1]] — Point72 expects US growth next year of about 1.5% or lower, which he calls okay but not phenomenal.

## [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328]]
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C15-3|MT155-C15-3]] — The dollar combines sentiment, monetary policy and US growth; the pro-growth outlook and cautious Fed support the trend short-term but the long-run is unclear.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C16-1|MT155-C16-1]] — Cyclically the dollar is positive: US animal spirits may force the Fed to raise rates again while Europe cuts.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C16-3|MT155-C16-3]] — A Plaza-type accord (as in 1985) could be used for a coordinated dollar decline if the dollar overshoots further.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C16-4|MT155-C16-4]] — The yield curve may steepen (a 2024 prediction that held).
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C17-1|MT155-C17-1]] — After tariff bluster, a Trump-China compromise like Nixon's would revive Chinese assets relative to the rest of the world, a big 2025 trade.

## [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151]]
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C03-1|MT156-C03-1]] — With too little inflation the Fed has no conflict between its mandates (easing helps both), which made bonds powerful diversifiers; with too much inflation there is no riskless policy path and that diversification weakens.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C03-3|MT156-C03-3]] — If the Fed tolerates about 3% inflation while saying its target is 2%, credibility suffers and investors may not be compensated for that inflation in long bonds (term premium).
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C04-3|MT156-C04-3]] — The affordability problem (real income lagging the price level) cannot be fixed by lower prices (deflationary spiral) so the political resolution needs nominal income growth, which is inflationary.

## [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421]]
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C09-1|MT157-C09-1]] — In the 2000-02 bust (S&P -40%, Nasdaq -80%) US Agg gained 30%, REITs 49%, gold and GSCI rose and the SG CTA index 26% because the Fed cut to the floor, setting the diversifier playbook.

## [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304]]
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C05-X1|MT158-C05-X1]] — Market-expected policy moves for 2024 repriced from six at the start of the year to about one (caption says 'hikes', context implies expected cuts), which sustained the short-bond trade until prices began to rally in mid-2024.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C13-5|MT158-C13-5]] — In the post-GFC 'CTA winter' (central bank intervention, Fed put), alternative markets did well while traditional futures struggled.

## [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger]]
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C01-1|MT159-C01-1]] — Several large policy changes (tariffs, China embargo, tax cliff, deficit cuts, wars) run simultaneously, so the probability of a smooth landing on all of them is the product of individually discounted odds and is low, which raises market uncertainty.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C02-3|MT159-C02-3]] — Two views of tariffs frame the market outcome: a tactical 'fully loaded gun rarely discharged' approach to reshore critical industries versus the Navarro approach of high structural tariffs replacing income tax.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C02-4|MT159-C02-4]] — Equities fell about 15% after the April 2 tariff announcement and China imposed a rare earth export ban on April 5, followed by a US ban on Nvidia H20 chips, showing escalation from tariffs to export controls.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C03-X1|MT159-C03-X1]] — The Smoot-Hawley tariff of the 1930s is the historical evidence that broad tariffs deepen downturns and delay recovery.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C03-X2|MT159-C03-X2]] — Tariffs create a spiral: protected domestic firms stop innovating, foreign retaliation triggers trade wars, prices rise and demand falls, markets shrink and jobs are lost.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C03-X4|MT159-C03-X4]] — Tariff escalation puts the 10-year Treasury and the dollar under stress, which makes Congress, including Republicans who have historically opposed tariffs, push back and raises the chance of policy reversal.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C04-2|MT159-C04-2]] — Inconsistent trade policy stops firms from adjusting supply chains, e.g. companies that moved from China to Vietnam under the first Trump administration now face tariffs there.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C05-1|MT159-C05-1]] — A narrow targeted policy (chips, pharma, rare earths) plus an accelerationist agenda of tax incentives is the lower-risk path to reshoring, while nuclear-scale tariffs on every country are a mismatch to that objective.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C05-X3|MT159-C05-X3]] — An unclear policy objective (tactical reshoring versus Navarro-scale tariffs) means broad bipartisan opposition if the larger version is intended, which adds to market uncertainty.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-2|MT159-C07-2]] — Tariffs and export controls typically trigger retaliation and escalation; each retaliatory tariff is like shooting a second hole in one's own boat (Friedman).
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-X1|MT159-C07-X1]] — A presidential signal that China tariffs will be unilaterally walked down from the 140% peak is treated as an off-ramp from the escalation.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-X2|MT159-C07-X2]] — The banned H20 was a deliberately downgraded chip two or three generations behind US products, so banning it gave little security benefit while costing sales.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-X3|MT159-C07-X3]] — A company that complies with one export-control rule can be hit by the next, as with Nvidia's H20 and with firms that moved from China to Vietnam, so government rule changes are an uncontrollable single-stock risk.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C08-X2|MT159-C08-X2]] — Gerstner separates frontier chips (restrict) from the older H20 (allow): selling H20 kept Chinese firms on CUDA and denied Huawei monopoly profits, so banning it was self-defeating.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C08-X4|MT159-C08-X4]] — Complex policy often fails and sometimes backfires even inside the US; export controls are external regulation over a world where the US controls few pieces, so the failure risk is higher.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C09-1|MT159-C09-1]] — Alienating allies with tariffs and pressure on firms such as ASML or TSMC raises the risk that foreign firms refuse to comply, hurting US supply chains.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C09-2|MT159-C09-2]] — Reaching a trade deal in 3-4 weeks would require unilateral US concessions framed as victory, such as walking China tariffs back to 30-34% and suspending the H20 ban for 90 days.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C09-X1|MT159-C09-X1]] — Proponents argue that previous (Biden) chip rules failed and therefore need tightening; this is circular, because controls that leaked before will leak again.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C10-X4|MT159-C10-X4]] — Export-control churn imposes a compliance and attention cost on US executives that diverts effort from building.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C11-1|MT159-C11-1]] — A US ban on DeepSeek would push Europe and South America to adopt it and speed Huawei chip and model optimization, so it would likely backfire.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C11-2|MT159-C11-2]] — If the US wants other countries to run on US compute and models, tariffing allies at the same time as China drives them to negotiate with China instead (EU, Japan, Korea, Vietnam, Middle East).
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C11-3|MT159-C11-3]] — The decision to be in the market depends on whether the president is a tactical free trader or a Navarro-style structural tariffer; Gerstner believes the former.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C12-1|MT159-C12-1]] — Export bans on US products such as H20 chips raise the US trade deficit with China (about $15B of H20 sales) and cost Nvidia about $5B in write-offs, contradicting the aim of narrowing trade imbalances.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C12-X1|MT159-C12-X1]] — Roughly $15B of H20 sales yields $8-9B of Nvidia earnings and $2-3B of US corporate tax, so banning the sale gives up profit that funded Nvidia's lead and Treasury revenue while Huawei's chips already exceed the H20.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C13-X1|MT159-C13-X1]] — If AI is treated as existential to national security, blocking a rival's access to it is an aggressive act and invites escalation.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C15-3|MT159-C15-3]] — Gerstner expects deals to land with 5-10% tariffs on most countries (about $200B) and around 25-30% on China, so he leans into risk-off down days.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C16-X2|MT159-C16-X2]] — Trade-deal frameworks (India: terms of reference only) take months, far longer than the 3 to 4 weeks the president claimed, so uncertainty will outlast the market's patience.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C16-X3|MT159-C16-X3]] — Market recovery needs predictability: a credible four-year commitment to narrow reshoring (not Navarro-style replacement of income tax) and concluded deals with China, Japan and India would let companies plan.

## [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301]]
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C03-X3|MT160-C03-X3]] — After a very strong Q1, trend followers had a difficult spring: fixed income was whipsawed by inflation data and hopes of rate cuts (Europe cutting, the Fed holding out), and metals were hard; Europe's equities lagged the US after the European elections.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C09-1|MT160-C09-1]] — VIX futures for November and December price below October because of the US election on 5 November, so event risk shows up as a kink in the term structure that changes carry signals when rolling.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C11-2|MT160-C11-2]] — The 2024 US election was finely balanced, and markets (VIX in October) priced a Trump win as the riskier outcome.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C13-X2|MT160-C13-X2]] — Niels's caveat to Rob: many Trump-era policies were kept under Biden, so campaign rhetoric overstates the policy change an election produces.

## [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo]]
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C01-3|MT161-C01-3]] — The macro risks named are the war and oil, tariffs returning, hyperscaler capex debate and the Fed, which together make forward progress hard for the market.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C02-5|MT161-C02-5]] — The 10-year yield level that triggered equity corrections in 2023-25 was about 4.5%, but the threshold now looks higher.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C03-4|MT161-C03-4]] — A Fed that tightens because of strong growth rather than inflation is a different, less equity-hostile regime than 2022.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C03-5|MT161-C03-5]] — The recent rise in yields is in real rates rather than inflation breakevens, which argues against Fed tightening.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C03-6|MT161-C03-6]] — Detailed Fed communication (statements since 1994, press conferences since 2019) removes risk and encourages speculation, so cutting forward guidance should raise volatility and cut risk-taking.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C03-7|MT161-C03-7]] — At full employment the Fed's reaction function is inflation, so the Fed will respond to inflation prints rather than growth.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C03-X2|MT161-C03-X2]] — The new Fed leadership intends to end the 15-16 years of near-zero rates that favored financial markets and to set policy for the whole economy, so the Fed will be less supportive of asset prices and markets must adjust.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C08-X1|MT161-C08-X1]] — Washington's propensity to slow AI through regulation (with New York cited, comparable to what was done to fracking) is a policy risk for the AI build-out, which is why big tech invests in government relations.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C09-2|MT161-C09-2]] — Lobbying and Washington relations can generate alpha because government increasingly shapes the AI industry.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C09-3|MT161-C09-3]] — Banks globally (US, Japanese, European, brokers) are leaders, and the private credit scare did not become a systemic banking threat; double-B spreads hit new cycle lows.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C09-4|MT161-C09-4]] — Deregulation of banks is a precondition for shrinking the Fed balance sheet and supports bank stocks.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C09-X1|MT161-C09-X1]] — Hundreds of social-media harm lawsuits (two already lost by Meta) add legal overhang and a possible earnings drag; individually small versus revenue, they accumulate.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-4|MT161-C11-4]] — Regulated utilities have been weak and making new lows, and unregulated power producers are weakening too, which the speaker attributes to politics consuming the sector.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C12-3|MT161-C12-3]] — Increasingly populist politics produces a reaction against large institutions, creating policy risk for large pharma, banks, oil and others.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C12-X2|MT161-C12-X2]] — Health care benefited for years from the expanding role of government, but pricing pressure now runs the other way (any pricing action reduces revenue), a policy headwind despite the chart strength.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C13-1|MT161-C13-1]] — With the Fed no longer sowing certainty, markets will see higher volatility while good companies are still rewarded.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C13-2|MT161-C13-2]] — Eisman's summary: strategists expected a 4.5% 10-year to hurt stocks but it did not, so they now say the 10-year must go significantly higher before a correction, and they stay bullish unless that or an AI collapse happens.

## [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield]]
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C04-1|MT162-C04-1]] — Covid caused a 35% US equity fall in six weeks, after which central banks cut rates to zero and restarted QE.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C04-X2|MT162-C04-X2]] — Alongside central bank easing, governments launched massive fiscal support (furloughs, bill payment, mortgage holidays, business loans) explicitly to avoid a repeat of the 1930s depression, so the policy response to a shock is now fiscal as well as monetary.

## [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357]]
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C07-1|MT163-C07-1]] — Political pressure on the Fed (Powell as with Nixon and Burns) is expected to bring dramatic bond moves, a steepener, a 'debt jubilee' within 5-10 years and cross-country currency volatility (yen-dollar carry blowup last August).
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C09-2|MT163-C09-2]] — Tariff costs hit corporate margins first, then prices; earnings and guidance show it before CPI, so watch margin compression, then inflation, then employment.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C09-X1|MT163-C09-X1]] — Q2 earnings reflect only soft tariffs because many were rolled back; the full margin and inflation effect has not yet shown, so guidance is the key release.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C10-1|MT163-C10-1]] — CPI includes imports while PPI does not, so a CPI-PPI divergence is informative while tariffs apply.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C11-X1|MT163-C11-X1]] — Because the Fed is data-dependent, an administration that wants rate cuts has an incentive to shape the macro data (CPI, payrolls), so official releases deserve extra scepticism while Powell is under pressure.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C11-X2|MT163-C11-X2]] — Cutting BLS resources forced more reliance on estimates (historically 10% or lower, now 30-35% of CPI), turning the data into a qualitative judgement made by appointees.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C11-X3|MT163-C11-X3]] — The shift to citing Federal Reserve renovation cost overruns is read as building a 'cause' to remove Powell, which would otherwise require cause.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C12-1|MT163-C12-1]] — A credible threat to fire Powell (steepener: front down, back up) is more useful than acting, because firing would raise long yields and the board still votes on rates; markets reacted at once to the letter news.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C12-2|MT163-C12-2]] — To get rate cuts before the midterms, the administration needs a slowdown and lower markets (a controlled demolition), so a market decline may be tolerated.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C12-X1|MT163-C12-X1]] — A credible but unexecuted threat to remove Powell acts as a shadow-Fed move: markets price in easier policy at once, supporting equities and controlling the narrative while hedge funds are short.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C15-1|MT163-C15-1]] — Holding long-term rates below inflation causes borrowing to buy real assets and pull forward demand, an inflation spiral as in the 1970s.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C19-1|MT163-C19-1]] — Cuts to regulators are said to enable data control and favor wealthy corporations.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C19-X1|MT163-C19-X1]] — Paul Tudor Jones reportedly sees libertarianism, i.e. no safeguards on AI, as as big a threat as socialism, which Niels links to AI regulation moratoria.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C21-X3|MT163-C21-X3]] — The decisive data (earnings, inflation, employment, Powell/Trump outcome) arrive within two months and decide how the relief rally resolves.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C22-X1|MT163-C22-X1]] — Authorities are expected to cushion any decline, which compresses volatility and makes the decline a slow, controlled one.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C22-X2|MT163-C22-X2]] — Political timeline: midterms and a new Fed chair in June 2026 push the administration to want rate cuts and a positive market by then, which shapes the scenarios.

## [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359]]
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C02-1|MT164-C02-1]] — At the July 2025 FOMC two dissenters (Waller, Bowman), both candidates to replace Powell, favored a cut while the chair showed no hurry, pointing to political pressure on the Fed.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C02-X1|MT164-C02-X1]] — Historically Fed chairs set fiscal conditions for cuts (Greenspan 1990, Volcker 1985 asked for about a $50B deficit cut), unlike today when the president pressures the Fed.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C03-1|MT164-C03-1]] — US copper fell about 20% in a day after a change in the Trump tariff policy, hurting trend followers who held long copper after a breakout.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C03-3|MT164-C03-3]] — The Fed faces a dilemma between a slowing economy (inflation lower) and robust growth (inflation higher), so it takes a precautionary stance because tariff pass-through is unknown.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C03-X1|MT164-C03-X1]] — Doubt about Fed independence weighs on fixed-income markets and adds to the difficulty of knowing the Fed's reaction function.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C05-3|MT164-C05-3]] — Trend profits in fixed income reflect long central bank policy cycles, which suit trend following, whereas equity trends are harder because of sudden short-term volatility.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C06-X1|MT164-C06-X1]] — Identify the dominant player per market: central banks dominate fixed income (even more than before), so trend in bonds is a trend in central-bank behaviour and the strategy is exposed to a change of central-bank view.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C08-2|MT164-C08-2]] — Less muted political statements cause fast shocks; systematic traders must react, are pulled away from do-nothing, and get whipsawed when statements reverse.

## [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360]]
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C01-1|MT165-C01-1]] — TIPS are priced on the actual CPI print, so tampering with BLS data (after the BLS head was fired) could raise the risk premium demanded in TIPS, though no meaningful reaction was seen.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C03-1|MT165-C03-1]] — The 39% US tariff on Switzerland may overstate a trade deficit that largely reflects gold flows rather than goods trade.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C03-2|MT165-C03-2]] — The first six months of Trump's policy produced a much slower economy than expected (tariff tax, consumer spending at about 1% annualized versus 2% normal), contrary to the run-it-hot expectation.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C03-X1|MT165-C03-X1]] — Counterpoint: replacing BLS leadership could be a legitimate attempt to fix declining data quality (low survey response, imputation), so the firing is not proof of manipulation.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C04-2|MT165-C04-2]] — Weak payrolls and negative revisions made a 25bp September cut near-certain, with a bigger cut possible if CPI did not rise.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C04-X2|MT165-C04-X2]] — The expected pro-growth levers of the new administration (fiscal stimulus via the big beautiful bill, deregulation) have not shown up in the data, so only tariffs and immigration effects are visible; some stimulus may come in 2026.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C04-X4|MT165-C04-X4]] — Because labour supply and demand are both shrinking, the unemployment rate stays near 4.2% even as job growth and demand weaken, which keeps the Fed's mandate gauge from signalling urgency and makes the policy choice hard.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C05-X1|MT165-C05-X1]] — The Fed treats the unemployment rate as its most important labour indicator, which simplifies its reaction function despite GDP/income mismeasurement.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C06-1|MT165-C06-1]] — Under ample reserves the fed funds market no longer signals the stance of policy, motivating Jeremy Siegel's call for Fed reform; the Fed is also criticised from both hard-money and easy-money sides.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C06-2|MT165-C06-2]] — The choice of the next Fed chair matters for the policy path because the chair sets the agenda, even with only one vote.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C06-X1|MT165-C06-X1]] — Critics from the hard-money side say the Fed missed the 2021 inflation surge and drifted from its mandate (climate, inclusive employment), and Bessent and Warsh call for root-and-branch reform, so institutional change at the Fed is a live risk.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C07-1|MT165-C07-1]] — Candidate Kevin Warsh is hard-money: opposes ongoing QE, favors lower rates on an AI productivity boom, and criticizes the Fed for facilitating fiscal profligacy; Fed cut 100bp in 2024 yet long yields rose 100bp.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C07-X1|MT165-C07-X1]] — Recent Fed chairs (Powell, and candidate Warsh) are not academic economists, so the appointee is more of a political actor and Fed policy more exposed to political aims.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C08-1|MT165-C08-1]] — A Warsh-style Fed (monetary aggregates, credit growth, no asset purchases or bailouts) would be a radical departure and could be more challenging for equity markets if it finances neither the deficit nor markets.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C08-X2|MT165-C08-X2]] — Warsh attributes the 2021 inflation entirely to the Fed and denies supply-shock effects; Niels agrees the Fed was out of touch but says supply issues mattered.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C09-2|MT165-C09-2]] — Central bank independence follows cycles; currently strong politicians and weak central banks, with hedge-fund titans influential in policy.

## [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361]]
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C04-X3|MT166-C04-X3]] — The deglobalisation, protectionism and central-bank-divergence thesis for trend following has not paid off yet because erratic US policy noise currently swamps the underlying trends; systems may need to treat the noise as the signal.

## [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392]]
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C04-4|MT167-C04-4]] — Powell distinguishes today from 1970s stagflation by unemployment levels, but rates have moved higher and cuts the White House wants may not come.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C05-1|MT167-C05-1]] — Oil shocks feed inflation nowcasts, memories of 2022 return, and stagflation risk is judged elevated versus two months earlier; the key unknown is whether the oil shock is permanent.

## [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393]]
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C04-X3|MT168-C04-X3]] — Niels notes apparent trading in oil and equities just ahead of policy announcements (S&P selling off, then rallying before a Trump statement), suggesting event-driven reversals can be front-run and are hard for a rules-based process to anticipate; both speakers defer the market-manipulation topic.

## [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418]]
